No one has sat me down and explained why the calls go strange, or why certain people seem to know what I was going to say before I say it, or why invitations and messages come in these odd patterns right after I’ve been talking about the archive. They just tell me not to think that way.\n\nBut I do think it’s true. I feel very sure about it. I know others don’t believe me, or they pretend not to, but that doesn’t make it untrue.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a big part of it. It’s not just that I don’t get around to things, it’s that the things themselves don’t seem to give me that little spark anymore.\n\nLike with my herbs by the kitchen window — I used to really enjoy that. Not in some grand way, just… it was pleasant. The basil, the thyme, touching the soil, checking if they needed water. It made the kitchen feel alive. Now I’ll sit at the table and look right at the pots drying out, and I know they need me, but I don’t feel much of anything. Not pleasure, not urgency, not even the small satisfaction I used to get from taking care of them.\n\nAnd friends will invite me to things I would have loved before — a concert, a gallery opening, something with music or art, people talking afterward. Those used to make me feel interested, like I wanted to be there. Now I see the message and it’s like it lands flat. I know I should want to go. I know the old version of me would have called back, picked an outfit, asked who else was coming. But now I just don’t feel the pull.\n\nSo yes, I’m not enjoying things the way I used to. It’s like the world is still offering the same things, but whatever part of me used to respond to them has gone quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're unable to initiate activities on your own?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so.\n\nIt’s like… I can know something needs doing, and it still doesn’t turn into movement. I’ll sit at my kitchen table for long stretches, sometimes with my tea gone cold, just looking at the herb pots by the window. I can see the soil is dry. I can see the basil has started to droop, and part of me knows, “You should get up, get the watering can, it would take less than two minutes.” But I don’t do it. I just sit there.\n\nAnd it’s not that I’m making some big decision not to. It isn’t laziness exactly, or even that I’m busy with something else. It’s more like the starting part doesn’t happen. The thought is there, but the push behind it is missing.\n\nIt happens with people too. A friend will leave a message, or someone will invite me to a concert or a gallery opening, and I may even think, “That sounds like something I used to go to.” I’ll picture answering them, or getting dressed, or looking up the time, but then I don’t call back. The message just sits there. Then more time passes, and it feels harder to respond because now I’d have to explain why I didn’t answer sooner.\n\nSo yes, I do have trouble initiating things on my own. Even small things. I can sit there for hours with everything waiting around me, and I just don’t begin.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like the voices or your thoughts were controlling your actions or telling you to do things that you didn't want to do?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was one time that really frightened people, I think. It was at the museum program, the one that had been postponed, and I had gone there already feeling like there was a message around it — not exactly a voice in my ear like someone standing next to me, but a thought that felt placed there, very firm, like I had been instructed.\n\nThe room was set up in this ordinary way, rows of chairs, very bland, like nothing had happened, like the postponement didn’t mean anything. But I felt that leaving the chairs like that was wrong. It felt like I was supposed to arrange them into a kind of island map, because that was the shape of what was being hidden. So I started moving them. I knew it probably looked strange, but at the time it didn’t feel optional. It felt like if I didn’t do it, I was disobeying the message I had been given.\n\nAnd then I stood at the front with my arms lifted. I wasn’t trying to make a scene exactly. I just felt… compelled. Like my body had to take that position so the message would be received properly. Part of me knew people might not understand, and I didn’t exactly want to be standing there like that, but stopping felt worse. It felt like I would be failing something important if I walked away.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voices or your thoughts are revealing a conspiracy or a plot against you that is not apparent to others?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know it sounds like a lot when I say it plainly, but yes, I do believe there’s a plot around the archive and the museum.\n\nIt isn’t just a vague feeling like, “Oh, people don’t like my work.” It’s more specific than that. I’m convinced the museum board and the city arts people have been monitoring my phone. Not in some dramatic movie way, but… they know things. They know when I’ve been talking about the Caribbean archive, and they know what it means. They understand that if the material is shown properly, it changes the whole story the galleries have been telling for years. It would make them admit what they’ve left out, what they’ve softened, what they’ve made decorative instead of truthful.\n\nAnd that is why I think they’ve been watching. Because they don’t want the galleries to tell the truth. They want to keep control of the version people see.\n\nPeople tell me there’s no proof, but no one has shown me anything that proves I’m wrong either. They just say, “That doesn’t make sense,” or “Why would they do that?” But that isn’t proof. That’s just them not wanting to look at it closely. So yes, I believe there’s something being hidden, and I believe they know exactly why my archive matters.\n\nDoctor: Do you often see things that aren't there, like shadows or shapes, in addition to hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the voices, not so much seeing things.\n\nI mean, what keeps bringing me in, what makes it hard to just dismiss it as stress or an overactive imagination, is that I hear them very clearly sometimes. In my apartment, especially when it’s quiet, I’ll hear women whispering like they’re just behind the walls. Not muffled in the way neighbors sound, but close and deliberate, like they’re discussing the archive and trying to tell me which photographs belong where.\n\nAnd it’s happened in empty museum rooms too. That’s the part that unsettles me, because I’ll know no one else is in the room, and still I can hear these whispers, like women speaking from the walls or from just beyond the room. They’ll be saying things about the photographs, which ones are important, which ones have been misplaced or hidden. It doesn’t feel random.\n\nAs for shadows or shapes… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say that’s the main thing. It’s really the hearing that is clear. The voices are what I notice, and they feel very present.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to keep your thoughts in order when speaking or thinking, even when there are no voices?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do think that happens, even when I’m not hearing anything. It’s embarrassing, because I’ll think I’m answering very clearly, like I’m giving all the pieces they need to understand, but then I see people’s faces and I realize I’ve gone somewhere else.\n\nLike if my cousin asks me something simple — “Did you pay that bill?” or “What time is the appointment?” — I might start answering, but then I’ll explain why the grant mattered, and then how the shipping routes connect to what was taken or moved, and then I’m talking about my grandmother’s altar because that’s where I first learned how things get preserved, and then somehow I’m back at the appointment but nobody knows what I meant anymore.\n\nIn my mind it feels connected. It doesn’t feel random while I’m saying it. Each piece leads to the next piece, like stepping stones. But I think other people don’t see the stones, so to them I sound like I’m wandering. And sometimes by the time I stop talking, I can’t even remember the original question exactly, or I know it was about something practical but I’ve made it into this whole… long explanation. My cousin will just look tired and say, “Can you just tell me yes or no?” and then I feel foolish, because I wasn’t trying to make it difficult.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think it does happen even when there aren’t voices pushing at me. It’s not only when I’m hearing something. Sometimes it’s just ordinary conversation, and I can feel myself trying to answer, but the answer sort of opens into too many rooms.

Like with my cousin — she’ll ask something very plain, like whether I paid a bill or what time an appointment is, and I’ll mean to say yes or no, or give the time. But then I start explaining why the grant mattered, and then I’m talking about shipping routes and where things were moved, and then somehow I’m explaining my grandmother’s altar because that feels connected to preservation and memory, and by the time I stop, nobody seems to know what I meant.

It feels connected to me while I’m saying it. That’s the frustrating part. I’m not trying to be confusing. But I can see from people’s faces that I’ve lost the thread, or maybe they have. And then I feel ashamed because it was supposed to be a simple answer.

  {

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23, F25.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came because I keep hearing voices, very clearly, and it’s been frightening and distracting. It happens mostly in my apartment, but also sometimes when I’m in empty rooms at the museum — especially the quieter rooms where the photographs are kept or displayed.\n\nIt’s like women whispering from behind the walls. Not a vague sound, not like the pipes or neighbors exactly… they sound like actual voices, and they tell me which photographs belong in the archive. I know that sounds strange when I say it out loud, but when it’s happening it feels very specific, like they’re discussing the collection and directing me. It’s gotten to the point where I can’t just ignore it, because I’ll be standing there trying to focus and then I hear them again, as if they’re right behind the wall or just out of sight.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself acting on the instructions or advice given by the voices?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes I do. I don’t always mean to, and afterward I can see how it must have looked, but in the moment it feels like I’ve been given something I’m supposed to follow.\n\nThere was this museum program that had been postponed, and I was there, and instead of just leaving or waiting like everyone else would, I felt like the message was that the room needed to be arranged a certain way. So I started moving the chairs around into… I don’t know how to explain it exactly, like an island map, with little clusters and spaces between them. It felt very specific, like the arrangement meant something, not just random chairs.\n\nAnd then I stood at the front with my arms lifted. I remember feeling embarrassed even while doing it, but also like if I stopped or walked away, I would be disobeying what I’d been told. It had this awful pressure to it, like the voices had given me a task and I couldn’t simply ignore it.\n\nDoctor: When you're not hearing the voices, do you find it difficult to express your thoughts or follow a conversation?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. It’s not only when the voices are happening. Even when it’s quiet, I can lose the line of what someone asked me.\n\nLike my cousin will ask me something very simple — just, “Did you pay that bill?” or “What time is the appointment?” — and I know there is a straight answer. I can feel there’s supposed to be a straight answer. But then I start explaining how the grant money was organized, and then I’m talking about shipping routes for the photographs, and then somehow I’m explaining my grandmother’s altar because it feels connected in my mind at the time.\n\nAnd I can hear myself going on, but I can’t always get back to the original question. By the time I stop, my cousin looks confused or tired, and nobody seems to know what I meant — sometimes I don’t even know if I answered the question. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be difficult. It just… spreads out in too many directions.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your thoughts are similarly disorganized and hard to follow when you're not hearing the voices and there's no specific task or decision you need to make?\n\nPatient: Yes… it happens even when there isn’t some big decision in front of me. It can be just an ordinary moment, really, and that almost makes it worse because there’s no reason for it to get so tangled.\n\nLike if my cousin asks me something plain — a bill, an appointment time, whether I handled something — I know she’s asking for one small piece of information. I know that. But when I open my mouth, it’s like the answer won’t stay in one place. I’ll start with the bill, and then somehow I’m explaining the grant money and how it was organized, and then I’m talking about shipping routes for photographs, because in my mind that feels connected, and then it drifts into my grandmother’s altar because that also feels connected somehow.\n\nAnd while I’m saying it, part of me can tell I’m getting far away from the question, but I can’t always pull it back. By the time I stop, my cousin is looking at me like she doesn’t know what I meant, and honestly sometimes I don’t know if I ever answered her. It’s embarrassing. It feels like my thoughts scatter into these long paths, and other people can’t follow where I’m going.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe things that might not be true, like the voices are telling you something that only you understand?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do believe that. I know how it sounds when I say it out loud, but it doesn’t feel like some passing thought to me. It feels like something I’ve understood because the voices point to it, and then things around me line up with it.\n\nI’m convinced the museum board and the city arts people have been monitoring my phone. Not just casually, not like ordinary gossip — I mean watching, listening, tracking what I’m saying and who I’m talking to. Because they know my Caribbean archive matters. They know it would change how the galleries tell the truth, how they explain what was taken and what was hidden and who got left out of the story.\n\nAnd people say, “Well, where’s your proof?” but no one has shown me anything that proves I’m wrong either. They act like I’m supposed to just trust that none of it is happening, but why would I, when the timing and the little signs keep matching up? It feels like they understand what I have, and they don’t want me to be the one to reveal it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty completing tasks or following a daily routine due to feeling disorganized?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. I don’t like admitting that, because I used to be someone people could rely on, especially around museum things and community events. I had a reputation for knowing the details, keeping papers in order, remembering who needed to be called. And now it’s like the whole structure collapses before I can even see where to begin.\n\nI’ve missed board meetings — not because I didn’t care, but because I couldn’t keep the time straight, or I’d get stuck trying to prepare one thing and then lose the thread of the whole day. Bills have been late or just… forgotten in a pile, even when I meant to handle them. Appointments get confused. Money becomes hard to track. I’ll think I’ve written something down somewhere, but then there are three notebooks and loose envelopes and emails I didn’t answer, and I can’t tell which one matters.\n\nAnd I think people noticed. Colleagues who used to trust me stopped trusting me in the same way. They’d speak more carefully, or go around me, or ask someone else to confirm things. That hurt. It still hurts, because I know why they did it, but it feels like losing my place in my own life.\n\nMy cousin has had to step in a lot — with appointments, with bills, with making sure I actually get places or don’t agree to something I can’t manage. And there was a museum administrator too who had to help with public events, because I wasn’t managing them safely on my own. I would get overwhelmed by the details and the people and the timing, and someone else had to sort of… contain it, I guess. Keep it from falling apart. So yes, daily routine and tasks have become very difficult for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel emotionally flat or detached from activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. More than I like to admit.\n\nThere are these long stretches where I’m just sitting at my kitchen table, and the whole afternoon will pass, and I haven’t really done anything. I’ll notice the herb pots by the window are drying out — basil, thyme, little things I used to fuss over — and even seeing them like that doesn’t really move me to get up right away. I used to enjoy tending to them. It felt grounding, like keeping something alive and fragrant in the house. Now I’ll just sit there looking at them, knowing they need water, and it’s like the feeling that should come doesn’t arrive.\n\nAnd people invite me to things, still. A concert, or a gallery opening, something I would have cared about before. Those were my places, you know? I used to feel a little spark about getting dressed, seeing the work, talking afterward. Now when someone calls or leaves a message, I often don’t call back. Not because I hate them. It’s more like the interest is behind glass. I know I should respond, I know the invitation is kind, but I feel flat, like there’s no pull in me toward it.\n\nSometimes I just sit there with the phone nearby and think, “I’ll call in a minute,” and then it’s evening. Or the next day. And then I’m embarrassed, so I wait longer. It makes me feel detached from the life I used to recognize as mine.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're lacking motivation to do things you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’s like the starting part of me has gone missing.\n\nThere are these long stretches where I’ll just be sitting at my kitchen table, and I can see the herb pots by the window drying out. I know they need water. I know it would take almost nothing to stand up, take the little can, give them what they need. And I used to do that without thinking — I liked it, actually. But now I just sit there and look at them, and the thought doesn’t turn into action. It just stays a thought.\n\nAnd it’s not only chores. Friends will call or leave messages, or someone will invite me to a concert or a gallery opening, something I would have cared about before. Those were things that used to make me feel connected, like I still had a place in the world. Now I see the message and I think, “I should call them back,” but I don’t. Not right then, and sometimes not later either. I don’t feel the pull toward it. I don’t feel much interest, even when I know, in my head, that I normally would.\n\nThen I feel ashamed, because people are being kind and I’m just… not responding. But even the shame doesn’t always get me moving. So yes, I’d say the motivation is very low. It’s like I’m watching my own life from the chair at the kitchen table, and I can’t quite make myself step into it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to keep your thoughts in order when speaking or thinking, even when there are no voices?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even when there aren’t voices, even when it’s just me trying to answer a normal question, my thoughts don’t stay in a line.\n\nMy cousin will ask something simple, like, “Did you pay that bill?” or “What time is the appointment?” And I know there’s supposed to be a short answer. I know that. But then I start trying to explain why the bill matters, and then I’m talking about the grant that was connected to something years ago, and then somehow I’m explaining shipping routes or museum deliveries, and then I’ll remember my grandmother’s altar — because in my mind there’s a connection, there is, it feels like there is — but by the time I stop, my cousin is just looking at me like she can’t find the point anymore.\n\nAnd I can feel it happening sometimes. Not always at first, but partway through I’ll realize I’ve gone off the main road, so to speak, but I don’t know how to get back. If I try to correct it, I add more explanation, and that makes it worse. I’ll say, “No, wait, what I mean is…” and then there’s another whole branch of it.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be difficult. I’m trying to be precise, actually. I’m trying to give the full context so the answer makes sense. But it comes out tangled, and then nobody seems to know what I meant — sometimes I don’t even know if I answered the question.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voices or your thoughts are telling you something that is true, even if others do not believe it?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, I do believe it, even though people keep acting like it’s just me “making connections.”\n\nIt’s about the museum board, and the city arts people — the ones around the galleries and funding and all of that. I’m convinced they’ve been monitoring my phone. Not in some vague way, not just “oh, everyone is watched these days,” but specifically because of my Caribbean archive. They know what’s in it, or at least they know enough to be worried. That archive changes things. It changes how the galleries would have to tell the truth, how they talk about where objects came from, who gets named, who gets left out, what gets made decorative instead of historical.\n\nAnd when I say that, people look at me like I’ve gone too far. They say, “What proof do you have?” But no one has shown me anything that proves they’re not doing it. No one has sat me down and explained why the calls go strange, or why certain people seem to know what I was going to say before I say it, or why invitations and messages come in these odd patterns right after I’ve been talking about the archive. They just tell me not to think that way.\n\nBut I do think it’s true. I feel very sure about it. I know others don’t believe me, or they pretend not to, but that doesn’t make it untrue.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a big part of it. It’s not just that I don’t get around to things, it’s that the things themselves don’t seem to give me that little spark anymore.\n\nLike with my herbs by the kitchen window — I used to really enjoy that. Not in some grand way, just… it was pleasant. The basil, the thyme, touching the soil, checking if they needed water. It made the kitchen feel alive. Now I’ll sit at the table and look right at the pots drying out, and I know they need me, but I don’t feel much of anything. Not pleasure, not urgency, not even the small satisfaction I used to get from taking care of them.\n\nAnd friends will invite me to things I would have loved before — a concert, a gallery opening, something with music or art, people talking afterward. Those used to make me feel interested, like I wanted to be there. Now I see the message and it’s like it lands flat. I know I should want to go. I know the old version of me would have called back, picked an outfit, asked who else was coming. But now I just don’t feel the pull.\n\nSo yes, I’m not enjoying things the way I used to. It’s like the world is still offering the same things, but whatever part of me used to respond to them has gone quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're unable to initiate activities on your own?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so.\n\nIt’s like… I can know something needs doing, and it still doesn’t turn into movement. I’ll sit at my kitchen table for long stretches, sometimes with my tea gone cold, just looking at the herb pots by the window. I can see the soil is dry. I can see the basil has started to droop, and part of me knows, “You should get up, get the watering can, it would take less than two minutes.” But I don’t do it. I just sit there.\n\nAnd it’s not that I’m making some big decision not to. It isn’t laziness exactly, or even that I’m busy with something else. It’s more like the starting part doesn’t happen. The thought is there, but the push behind it is missing.\n\nIt happens with people too. A friend will leave a message, or someone will invite me to a concert or a gallery opening, and I may even think, “That sounds like something I used to go to.” I’ll picture answering them, or getting dressed, or looking up the time, but then I don’t call back. The message just sits there. Then more time passes, and it feels harder to respond because now I’d have to explain why I didn’t answer sooner.\n\nSo yes, I do have trouble initiating things on my own. Even small things. I can sit there for hours with everything waiting around me, and I just don’t begin.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like the voices or your thoughts were controlling your actions or telling you to do things that you didn't want to do?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was one time that really frightened people, I think. It was at the museum program, the one that had been postponed, and I had gone there already feeling like there was a message around it — not exactly a voice in my ear like someone standing next to me, but a thought that felt placed there, very firm, like I had been instructed.\n\nThe room was set up in this ordinary way, rows of chairs, very bland, like nothing had happened, like the postponement didn’t mean anything. But I felt that leaving the chairs like that was wrong. It felt like I was supposed to arrange them into a kind of island map, because that was the shape of what was being hidden. So I started moving them. I knew it probably looked strange, but at the time it didn’t feel optional. It felt like if I didn’t do it, I was disobeying the message I had been given.\n\nAnd then I stood at the front with my arms lifted. I wasn’t trying to make a scene exactly. I just felt… compelled. Like my body had to take that position so the message would be received properly. Part of me knew people might not understand, and I didn’t exactly want to be standing there like that, but stopping felt worse. It felt like I would be failing something important if I walked away.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voices or your thoughts are revealing a conspiracy or a plot against you that is not apparent to others?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know it sounds like a lot when I say it plainly, but yes, I do believe there’s a plot around the archive and the museum.\n\nIt isn’t just a vague feeling like, “Oh, people don’t like my work.” It’s more specific than that. I’m convinced the museum board and the city arts people have been monitoring my phone. Not in some dramatic movie way, but… they know things. They know when I’ve been talking about the Caribbean archive, and they know what it means. They understand that if the material is shown properly, it changes the whole story the galleries have been telling for years. It would make them admit what they’ve left out, what they’ve softened, what they’ve made decorative instead of truthful.\n\nAnd that is why I think they’ve been watching. Because they don’t want the galleries to tell the truth. They want to keep control of the version people see.\n\nPeople tell me there’s no proof, but no one has shown me anything that proves I’m wrong either. They just say, “That doesn’t make sense,” or “Why would they do that?” But that isn’t proof. That’s just them not wanting to look at it closely. So yes, I believe there’s something being hidden, and I believe they know exactly why my archive matters.\n\nDoctor: Do you often see things that aren't there, like shadows or shapes, in addition to hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the voices, not so much seeing things.\n\nI mean, what keeps bringing me in, what makes it hard to just dismiss it as stress or an overactive imagination, is that I hear them very clearly sometimes. In my apartment, especially when it’s quiet, I’ll hear women whispering like they’re just behind the walls. Not muffled in the way neighbors sound, but close and deliberate, like they’re discussing the archive and trying to tell me which photographs belong where.\n\nAnd it’s happened in empty museum rooms too. That’s the part that unsettles me, because I’ll know no one else is in the room, and still I can hear these whispers, like women speaking from the walls or from just beyond the room. They’ll be saying things about the photographs, which ones are important, which ones have been misplaced or hidden. It doesn’t feel random.\n\nAs for shadows or shapes… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say that’s the main thing. It’s really the hearing that is clear. The voices are what I notice, and they feel very present.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to keep your thoughts in order when speaking or thinking, even when there are no voices?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do think that happens, even when I’m not hearing anything. It’s embarrassing, because I’ll think I’m answering very clearly, like I’m giving all the pieces they need to understand, but then I see people’s faces and I realize I’ve gone somewhere else.\n\nLike if my cousin asks me something simple — “Did you pay that bill?” or “What time is the appointment?” — I might start answering, but then I’ll explain why the grant mattered, and then how the shipping routes connect to what was taken or moved, and then I’m talking about my grandmother’s altar because that’s where I first learned how things get preserved, and then somehow I’m back at the appointment but nobody knows what I meant anymore.\n\nIn my mind it feels connected. It doesn’t feel random while I’m saying it. Each piece leads to the next piece, like stepping stones. But I think other people don’t see the stones, so to them I sound like I’m wandering. And sometimes by the time I stop talking, I can’t even remember the original question exactly, or I know it was about something practical but I’ve made it into this whole… long explanation. My cousin will just look tired and say, “Can you just tell me yes or no?” and then I feel foolish, because I wasn’t trying to make it difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your disorganized thinking and behavior happen most of the time, even when you're not hearing voices or feeling pressured by them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does happen even when there aren’t voices pushing at me. It’s not only when I’m hearing something. Sometimes it’s just ordinary conversation, and I can feel myself trying to answer, but the answer sort of opens into too many rooms.\n\nLike with my cousin — she’ll ask something very plain, like whether I paid a bill or what time an appointment is, and I’ll mean to say yes or no, or give the time. But then I start explaining why the grant mattered, and then I’m talking about shipping routes and where things were moved, and then somehow I’m explaining my grandmother’s altar because that feels connected to preservation and memory, and by the time I stop, nobody seems to know what I meant.\n\nIt feels connected to me while I’m saying it. That’s the frustrating part. I’m not trying to be confusing. But I can see from people’s faces that I’ve lost the thread, or maybe they have. And then I feel ashamed because it was supposed to be a simple answer.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I don’t feel much interest, even when I know, in my head, that I normally would.\n\nThen I feel ashamed, because people are being kind and I’m just… not responding. But even the shame doesn’t always get me moving. So yes, I’d say the motivation is very low. It’s like I’m watching my own life from the chair at the kitchen table, and I can’t quite make myself step into it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to keep your thoughts in order when speaking or thinking, even when there are no voices?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even when there aren’t voices, even when it’s just me trying to answer a normal question, my thoughts don’t stay in a line.\n\nMy cousin will ask something simple, like, “Did you pay that bill?” or “What time is the appointment?” And I know there’s supposed to be a short answer. I know that. But then I start trying to explain why the bill matters, and then I’m talking about the grant that was connected to something years ago, and then somehow I’m explaining shipping routes or museum deliveries, and then I’ll remember my grandmother’s altar — because in my mind there’s a connection, there is, it feels like there is — but by the time I stop, my cousin is just looking at me like she can’t find the point anymore.\n\nAnd I can feel it happening sometimes. Not always at first, but partway through I’ll realize I’ve gone off the main road, so to speak, but I don’t know how to get back. If I try to correct it, I add more explanation, and that makes it worse. I’ll say, “No, wait, what I mean is…” and then there’s another whole branch of it.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be difficult. I’m trying to be precise, actually. I’m trying to give the full context so the answer makes sense. But it comes out tangled, and then nobody seems to know what I meant — sometimes I don’t even know if I answered the question.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voices or your thoughts are telling you something that is true, even if others do not believe it?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, I do believe it, even though people keep acting like it’s just me “making connections.”\n\nIt’s about the museum board, and the city arts people — the ones around the galleries and funding and all of that. I’m convinced they’ve been monitoring my phone. Not in some vague way, not just “oh, everyone is watched these days,” but specifically because of my Caribbean archive. They know what’s in it, or at least they know enough to be worried. That archive changes things. It changes how the galleries would have to tell the truth, how they talk about where objects came from, who gets named, who gets left out, what gets made decorative instead of historical.\n\nAnd when I say that, people look at me like I’ve gone too far. They say, “What proof do you have?” But no one has shown me anything that proves they’re not doing it. No one has sat me down and explained why the calls go strange, or why certain people seem to know what I was going to say before I say it, or why invitations and messages come in these odd patterns right after I’ve been talking about the archive. They just tell me not to think that way.\n\nBut I do think it’s true. I feel very sure about it. I know others don’t believe me, or they pretend not to, but that doesn’t make it untrue.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a big part of it. It’s not just that I don’t get around to things, it’s that the things themselves don’t seem to give me that little spark anymore.\n\nLike with my herbs by the kitchen window — I used to really enjoy that. Not in some grand way, just… it was pleasant. The basil, the thyme, touching the soil, checking if they needed water. It made the kitchen feel alive. Now I’ll sit at the table and look right at the pots drying out, and I know they need me, but I don’t feel much of anything. Not pleasure, not urgency, not even the small satisfaction I used to get from taking care of them.\n\nAnd friends will invite me to things I would have loved before — a concert, a gallery opening, something with music or art, people talking afterward. Those used to make me feel interested, like I wanted to be there. Now I see the message and it’s like it lands flat. I know I should want to go. I know the old version of me would have called back, picked an outfit, asked who else was coming. But now I just don’t feel the pull.\n\nSo yes, I’m not enjoying things the way I used to. It’s like the world is still offering the same things, but whatever part of me used to respond to them has gone quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're unable to initiate activities on your own?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so.\n\nIt’s like… I can know something needs doing, and it still doesn’t turn into movement. I’ll sit at my kitchen table for long stretches, sometimes with my tea gone cold, just looking at the herb pots by the window. I can see the soil is dry. I can see the basil has started to droop, and part of me knows, “You should get up, get the watering can, it would take less than two minutes.” But I don’t do it. I just sit there.\n\nAnd it’s not that I’m making some big decision not to. It isn’t laziness exactly, or even that I’m busy with something else. It’s more like the starting part doesn’t happen. The thought is there, but the push behind it is missing.\n\nIt happens with people too. A friend will leave a message, or someone will invite me to a concert or a gallery opening, and I may even think, “That sounds like something I used to go to.” I’ll picture answering them, or getting dressed, or looking up the time, but then I don’t call back. The message just sits there. Then more time passes, and it feels harder to respond because now I’d have to explain why I didn’t answer sooner.\n\nSo yes, I do have trouble initiating things on my own. Even small things. I can sit there for hours with everything waiting around me, and I just don’t begin.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like the voices or your thoughts were controlling your actions or telling you to do things that you didn't want to do?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was one time that really frightened people, I think. It was at the museum program, the one that had been postponed, and I had gone there already feeling like there was a message around it — not exactly a voice in my ear like someone standing next to me, but a thought that felt placed there, very firm, like I had been instructed.\n\nThe room was set up in this ordinary way, rows of chairs, very bland, like nothing had happened, like the postponement didn’t mean anything. But I felt that leaving the chairs like that was wrong. It felt like I was supposed to arrange them into a kind of island map, because that was the shape of what was being hidden. So I started moving them. I knew it probably looked strange, but at the time it didn’t feel optional. It felt like if I didn’t do it, I was disobeying the message I had been given.\n\nAnd then I stood at the front with my arms lifted. I wasn’t trying to make a scene exactly. I just felt… compelled. Like my body had to take that position so the message would be received properly. Part of me knew people might not understand, and I didn’t exactly want to be standing there like that, but stopping felt worse. It felt like I would be failing something important if I walked away.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voices or your thoughts are revealing a conspiracy or a plot against you that is not apparent to others?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know it sounds like a lot when I say it plainly, but yes, I do believe there’s a plot around the archive and the museum.\n\nIt isn’t just a vague feeling like, “Oh, people don’t like my work.” It’s more specific than that. I’m convinced the museum board and the city arts people have been monitoring my phone. Not in some dramatic movie way, but… they know things. They know when I’ve been talking about the Caribbean archive, and they know what it means. They understand that if the material is shown properly, it changes the whole story the galleries have been telling for years. It would make them admit what they’ve left out, what they’ve softened, what they’ve made decorative instead of truthful.\n\nAnd that is why I think they’ve been watching. Because they don’t want the galleries to tell the truth. They want to keep control of the version people see.\n\nPeople tell me there’s no proof, but no one has shown me anything that proves I’m wrong either. They just say, “That doesn’t make sense,” or “Why would they do that?” But that isn’t proof. That’s just them not wanting to look at it closely. So yes, I believe there’s something being hidden, and I believe they know exactly why my archive matters.\n\nDoctor: Do you often see things that aren't there, like shadows or shapes, in addition to hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the voices, not so much seeing things.\n\nI mean, what keeps bringing me in, what makes it hard to just dismiss it as stress or an overactive imagination, is that I hear them very clearly sometimes. In my apartment, especially when it’s quiet, I’ll hear women whispering like they’re just behind the walls. Not muffled in the way neighbors sound, but close and deliberate, like they’re discussing the archive and trying to tell me which photographs belong where.\n\nAnd it’s happened in empty museum rooms too. That’s the part that unsettles me, because I’ll know no one else is in the room, and still I can hear these whispers, like women speaking from the walls or from just beyond the room. They’ll be saying things about the photographs, which ones are important, which ones have been misplaced or hidden. It doesn’t feel random.\n\nAs for shadows or shapes… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say that’s the main thing. It’s really the hearing that is clear. The voices are what I notice, and they feel very present.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to keep your thoughts in order when speaking or thinking, even when there are no voices?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do think that happens, even when I’m not hearing anything. It’s embarrassing, because I’ll think I’m answering very clearly, like I’m giving all the pieces they need to understand, but then I see people’s faces and I realize I’ve gone somewhere else.\n\nLike if my cousin asks me something simple — “Did you pay that bill?” or “What time is the appointment?” — I might start answering, but then I’ll explain why the grant mattered, and then how the shipping routes connect to what was taken or moved, and then I’m talking about my grandmother’s altar because that’s where I first learned how things get preserved, and then somehow I’m back at the appointment but nobody knows what I meant anymore.\n\nIn my mind it feels connected. It doesn’t feel random while I’m saying it. Each piece leads to the next piece, like stepping stones. But I think other people don’t see the stones, so to them I sound like I’m wandering. And sometimes by the time I stop talking, I can’t even remember the original question exactly, or I know it was about something practical but I’ve made it into this whole… long explanation. My cousin will just look tired and say, “Can you just tell me yes or no?” and then I feel foolish, because I wasn’t trying to make it difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your disorganized thinking and behavior happen most of the time, even when you're not hearing voices or feeling pressured by them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does happen even when there aren’t voices pushing at me. It’s not only when I’m hearing something. Sometimes it’s just ordinary conversation, and I can feel myself trying to answer, but the answer sort of opens into too many rooms.\n\nLike with my cousin — she’ll ask something very plain, like whether I paid a bill or what time an appointment is, and I’ll mean to say yes or no, or give the time. But then I start explaining why the grant mattered, and then I’m talking about shipping routes and where things were moved, and then somehow I’m explaining my grandmother’s altar because that feels connected to preservation and memory, and by the time I stop, nobody seems to know what I meant.\n\nIt feels connected to me while I’m saying it. That’s the frustrating part. I’m not trying to be confusing. But I can see from people’s faces that I’ve lost the thread, or maybe they have. And then I feel ashamed because it was supposed to be a simple answer.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yes. It’s… it’s caused real problems. I wish I could say it was only private, something that just happened in my head and then passed, but it hasn’t stayed contained like that.

I’ve lost people’s trust, especially colleagues from the museum and the board. I used to be seen as someone who could keep things organized, who would show up prepared, who knew where records were and what needed to happen next. And now I’ve missed board meetings, or I’ve arrived in such a state that people didn’t know whether I was actually able to participate. There were public events where I thought I could manage it, and then afterward someone had to gently tell me I had confused people or made them uncomfortable, and that is very hard to hear.

Money and appointments have been bad too. I’ve missed bills — not because I didn’t care, but because I would put them somewhere “safe,” or I would start trying to sort out why the timing mattered, and then the actual payment just wouldn’t happen. My cousin has had to step in with things like appointments and bills, and a museum administrator also had to help manage some of the public-facing things because I wasn’t handling them safely on my own anymore.

It’s humiliating, honestly. I’m 64 years old. I have an education. I’m not someone who never knew how to manage a calendar or speak at an event. But lately I can’t reliably keep track of things, or know when I’m making a situation worse, and other people have had to take over pieces of my life that I used to manage myself.

---------- turn 18 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23, F25.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came because I keep hearing voices, very clearly, and it’s been frightening and distracting. It happens mostly in my apartment, but also sometimes when I’m in empty rooms at the museum — especially the quieter rooms where the photographs are kept or displayed.\n\nIt’s like women whispering from behind the walls. Not a vague sound, not like the pipes or neighbors exactly… they sound like actual voices, and they tell me which photographs belong in the archive. I know that sounds strange when I say it out loud, but when it’s happening it feels very specific, like they’re discussing the collection and directing me. It’s gotten to the point where I can’t just ignore it, because I’ll be standing there trying to focus and then I hear them again, as if they’re right behind the wall or just out of sight.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself acting on the instructions or advice given by the voices?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes I do. I don’t always mean to, and afterward I can see how it must have looked, but in the moment it feels like I’ve been given something I’m supposed to follow.\n\nThere was this museum program that had been postponed, and I was there, and instead of just leaving or waiting like everyone else would, I felt like the message was that the room needed to be arranged a certain way. So I started moving the chairs around into… I don’t know how to explain it exactly, like an island map, with little clusters and spaces between them. It felt very specific, like the arrangement meant something, not just random chairs.\n\nAnd then I stood at the front with my arms lifted. I remember feeling embarrassed even while doing it, but also like if I stopped or walked away, I would be disobeying what I’d been told. It had this awful pressure to it, like the voices had given me a task and I couldn’t simply ignore it.\n\nDoctor: When you're not hearing the voices, do you find it difficult to express your thoughts or follow a conversation?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. It’s not only when the voices are happening. Even when it’s quiet, I can lose the line of what someone asked me.\n\nLike my cousin will ask me something very simple — just, “Did you pay that bill?” or “What time is the appointment?” — and I know there is a straight answer. I can feel there’s supposed to be a straight answer. But then I start explaining how the grant money was organized, and then I’m talking about shipping routes for the photographs, and then somehow I’m explaining my grandmother’s altar because it feels connected in my mind at the time.\n\nAnd I can hear myself going on, but I can’t always get back to the original question. By the time I stop, my cousin looks confused or tired, and nobody seems to know what I meant — sometimes I don’t even know if I answered the question. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be difficult. It just… spreads out in too many directions.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your thoughts are similarly disorganized and hard to follow when you're not hearing the voices and there's no specific task or decision you need to make?\n\nPatient: Yes… it happens even when there isn’t some big decision in front of me. It can be just an ordinary moment, really, and that almost makes it worse because there’s no reason for it to get so tangled.\n\nLike if my cousin asks me something plain — a bill, an appointment time, whether I handled something — I know she’s asking for one small piece of information. I know that. But when I open my mouth, it’s like the answer won’t stay in one place. I’ll start with the bill, and then somehow I’m explaining the grant money and how it was organized, and then I’m talking about shipping routes for photographs, because in my mind that feels connected, and then it drifts into my grandmother’s altar because that also feels connected somehow.\n\nAnd while I’m saying it, part of me can tell I’m getting far away from the question, but I can’t always pull it back. By the time I stop, my cousin is looking at me like she doesn’t know what I meant, and honestly sometimes I don’t know if I ever answered her. It’s embarrassing. It feels like my thoughts scatter into these long paths, and other people can’t follow where I’m going.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe things that might not be true, like the voices are telling you something that only you understand?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do believe that. I know how it sounds when I say it out loud, but it doesn’t feel like some passing thought to me. It feels like something I’ve understood because the voices point to it, and then things around me line up with it.\n\nI’m convinced the museum board and the city arts people have been monitoring my phone. Not just casually, not like ordinary gossip — I mean watching, listening, tracking what I’m saying and who I’m talking to. Because they know my Caribbean archive matters. They know it would change how the galleries tell the truth, how they explain what was taken and what was hidden and who got left out of the story.\n\nAnd people say, “Well, where’s your proof?” but no one has shown me anything that proves I’m wrong either. They act like I’m supposed to just trust that none of it is happening, but why would I, when the timing and the little signs keep matching up? It feels like they understand what I have, and they don’t want me to be the one to reveal it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty completing tasks or following a daily routine due to feeling disorganized?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. I don’t like admitting that, because I used to be someone people could rely on, especially around museum things and community events. I had a reputation for knowing the details, keeping papers in order, remembering who needed to be called. And now it’s like the whole structure collapses before I can even see where to begin.\n\nI’ve missed board meetings — not because I didn’t care, but because I couldn’t keep the time straight, or I’d get stuck trying to prepare one thing and then lose the thread of the whole day. Bills have been late or just… forgotten in a pile, even when I meant to handle them. Appointments get confused. Money becomes hard to track. I’ll think I’ve written something down somewhere, but then there are three notebooks and loose envelopes and emails I didn’t answer, and I can’t tell which one matters.\n\nAnd I think people noticed. Colleagues who used to trust me stopped trusting me in the same way. They’d speak more carefully, or go around me, or ask someone else to confirm things. That hurt. It still hurts, because I know why they did it, but it feels like losing my place in my own life.\n\nMy cousin has had to step in a lot — with appointments, with bills, with making sure I actually get places or don’t agree to something I can’t manage. And there was a museum administrator too who had to help with public events, because I wasn’t managing them safely on my own. I would get overwhelmed by the details and the people and the timing, and someone else had to sort of… contain it, I guess. Keep it from falling apart. So yes, daily routine and tasks have become very difficult for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel emotionally flat or detached from activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. More than I like to admit.\n\nThere are these long stretches where I’m just sitting at my kitchen table, and the whole afternoon will pass, and I haven’t really done anything. I’ll notice the herb pots by the window are drying out — basil, thyme, little things I used to fuss over — and even seeing them like that doesn’t really move me to get up right away. I used to enjoy tending to them. It felt grounding, like keeping something alive and fragrant in the house. Now I’ll just sit there looking at them, knowing they need water, and it’s like the feeling that should come doesn’t arrive.\n\nAnd people invite me to things, still. A concert, or a gallery opening, something I would have cared about before. Those were my places, you know? I used to feel a little spark about getting dressed, seeing the work, talking afterward. Now when someone calls or leaves a message, I often don’t call back. Not because I hate them. It’s more like the interest is behind glass. I know I should respond, I know the invitation is kind, but I feel flat, like there’s no pull in me toward it.\n\nSometimes I just sit there with the phone nearby and think, “I’ll call in a minute,” and then it’s evening. Or the next day. And then I’m embarrassed, so I wait longer. It makes me feel detached from the life I used to recognize as mine.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're lacking motivation to do things you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’s like the starting part of me has gone missing.\n\nThere are these long stretches where I’ll just be sitting at my kitchen table, and I can see the herb pots by the window drying out. I know they need water. I know it would take almost nothing to stand up, take the little can, give them what they need. And I used to do that without thinking — I liked it, actually. But now I just sit there and look at them, and the thought doesn’t turn into action. It just stays a thought.\n\nAnd it’s not only chores. Friends will call or leave messages, or someone will invite me to a concert or a gallery opening, something I would have cared about before. Those were things that used to make me feel connected, like I still had a place in the world. Now I see the message and I think, “I should call them back,” but I don’t. Not right then, and sometimes not later either. I don’t feel the pull toward it. I don’t feel much interest, even when I know, in my head, that I normally would.\n\nThen I feel ashamed, because people are being kind and I’m just… not responding. But even the shame doesn’t always get me moving. So yes, I’d say the motivation is very low. It’s like I’m watching my own life from the chair at the kitchen table, and I can’t quite make myself step into it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to keep your thoughts in order when speaking or thinking, even when there are no voices?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even when there aren’t voices, even when it’s just me trying to answer a normal question, my thoughts don’t stay in a line.\n\nMy cousin will ask something simple, like, “Did you pay that bill?” or “What time is the appointment?” And I know there’s supposed to be a short answer. I know that. But then I start trying to explain why the bill matters, and then I’m talking about the grant that was connected to something years ago, and then somehow I’m explaining shipping routes or museum deliveries, and then I’ll remember my grandmother’s altar — because in my mind there’s a connection, there is, it feels like there is — but by the time I stop, my cousin is just looking at me like she can’t find the point anymore.\n\nAnd I can feel it happening sometimes. Not always at first, but partway through I’ll realize I’ve gone off the main road, so to speak, but I don’t know how to get back. If I try to correct it, I add more explanation, and that makes it worse. I’ll say, “No, wait, what I mean is…” and then there’s another whole branch of it.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be difficult. I’m trying to be precise, actually. I’m trying to give the full context so the answer makes sense. But it comes out tangled, and then nobody seems to know what I meant — sometimes I don’t even know if I answered the question.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voices or your thoughts are telling you something that is true, even if others do not believe it?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, I do believe it, even though people keep acting like it’s just me “making connections.”\n\nIt’s about the museum board, and the city arts people — the ones around the galleries and funding and all of that. I’m convinced they’ve been monitoring my phone. Not in some vague way, not just “oh, everyone is watched these days,” but specifically because of my Caribbean archive. They know what’s in it, or at least they know enough to be worried. That archive changes things. It changes how the galleries would have to tell the truth, how they talk about where objects came from, who gets named, who gets left out, what gets made decorative instead of historical.\n\nAnd when I say that, people look at me like I’ve gone too far. They say, “What proof do you have?” But no one has shown me anything that proves they’re not doing it. No one has sat me down and explained why the calls go strange, or why certain people seem to know what I was going to say before I say it, or why invitations and messages come in these odd patterns right after I’ve been talking about the archive. They just tell me not to think that way.\n\nBut I do think it’s true. I feel very sure about it. I know others don’t believe me, or they pretend not to, but that doesn’t make it untrue.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a big part of it. It’s not just that I don’t get around to things, it’s that the things themselves don’t seem to give me that little spark anymore.\n\nLike with my herbs by the kitchen window — I used to really enjoy that. Not in some grand way, just… it was pleasant. The basil, the thyme, touching the soil, checking if they needed water. It made the kitchen feel alive. Now I’ll sit at the table and look right at the pots drying out, and I know they need me, but I don’t feel much of anything. Not pleasure, not urgency, not even the small satisfaction I used to get from taking care of them.\n\nAnd friends will invite me to things I would have loved before — a concert, a gallery opening, something with music or art, people talking afterward. Those used to make me feel interested, like I wanted to be there. Now I see the message and it’s like it lands flat. I know I should want to go. I know the old version of me would have called back, picked an outfit, asked who else was coming. But now I just don’t feel the pull.\n\nSo yes, I’m not enjoying things the way I used to. It’s like the world is still offering the same things, but whatever part of me used to respond to them has gone quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're unable to initiate activities on your own?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so.\n\nIt’s like… I can know something needs doing, and it still doesn’t turn into movement. I’ll sit at my kitchen table for long stretches, sometimes with my tea gone cold, just looking at the herb pots by the window. I can see the soil is dry. I can see the basil has started to droop, and part of me knows, “You should get up, get the watering can, it would take less than two minutes.” But I don’t do it. I just sit there.\n\nAnd it’s not that I’m making some big decision not to. It isn’t laziness exactly, or even that I’m busy with something else. It’s more like the starting part doesn’t happen. The thought is there, but the push behind it is missing.\n\nIt happens with people too. A friend will leave a message, or someone will invite me to a concert or a gallery opening, and I may even think, “That sounds like something I used to go to.” I’ll picture answering them, or getting dressed, or looking up the time, but then I don’t call back. The message just sits there. Then more time passes, and it feels harder to respond because now I’d have to explain why I didn’t answer sooner.\n\nSo yes, I do have trouble initiating things on my own. Even small things. I can sit there for hours with everything waiting around me, and I just don’t begin.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like the voices or your thoughts were controlling your actions or telling you to do things that you didn't want to do?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was one time that really frightened people, I think. It was at the museum program, the one that had been postponed, and I had gone there already feeling like there was a message around it — not exactly a voice in my ear like someone standing next to me, but a thought that felt placed there, very firm, like I had been instructed.\n\nThe room was set up in this ordinary way, rows of chairs, very bland, like nothing had happened, like the postponement didn’t mean anything. But I felt that leaving the chairs like that was wrong. It felt like I was supposed to arrange them into a kind of island map, because that was the shape of what was being hidden. So I started moving them. I knew it probably looked strange, but at the time it didn’t feel optional. It felt like if I didn’t do it, I was disobeying the message I had been given.\n\nAnd then I stood at the front with my arms lifted. I wasn’t trying to make a scene exactly. I just felt… compelled. Like my body had to take that position so the message would be received properly. Part of me knew people might not understand, and I didn’t exactly want to be standing there like that, but stopping felt worse. It felt like I would be failing something important if I walked away.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voices or your thoughts are revealing a conspiracy or a plot against you that is not apparent to others?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know it sounds like a lot when I say it plainly, but yes, I do believe there’s a plot around the archive and the museum.\n\nIt isn’t just a vague feeling like, “Oh, people don’t like my work.” It’s more specific than that. I’m convinced the museum board and the city arts people have been monitoring my phone. Not in some dramatic movie way, but… they know things. They know when I’ve been talking about the Caribbean archive, and they know what it means. They understand that if the material is shown properly, it changes the whole story the galleries have been telling for years. It would make them admit what they’ve left out, what they’ve softened, what they’ve made decorative instead of truthful.\n\nAnd that is why I think they’ve been watching. Because they don’t want the galleries to tell the truth. They want to keep control of the version people see.\n\nPeople tell me there’s no proof, but no one has shown me anything that proves I’m wrong either. They just say, “That doesn’t make sense,” or “Why would they do that?” But that isn’t proof. That’s just them not wanting to look at it closely. So yes, I believe there’s something being hidden, and I believe they know exactly why my archive matters.\n\nDoctor: Do you often see things that aren't there, like shadows or shapes, in addition to hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the voices, not so much seeing things.\n\nI mean, what keeps bringing me in, what makes it hard to just dismiss it as stress or an overactive imagination, is that I hear them very clearly sometimes. In my apartment, especially when it’s quiet, I’ll hear women whispering like they’re just behind the walls. Not muffled in the way neighbors sound, but close and deliberate, like they’re discussing the archive and trying to tell me which photographs belong where.\n\nAnd it’s happened in empty museum rooms too. That’s the part that unsettles me, because I’ll know no one else is in the room, and still I can hear these whispers, like women speaking from the walls or from just beyond the room. They’ll be saying things about the photographs, which ones are important, which ones have been misplaced or hidden. It doesn’t feel random.\n\nAs for shadows or shapes… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say that’s the main thing. It’s really the hearing that is clear. The voices are what I notice, and they feel very present.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to keep your thoughts in order when speaking or thinking, even when there are no voices?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do think that happens, even when I’m not hearing anything. It’s embarrassing, because I’ll think I’m answering very clearly, like I’m giving all the pieces they need to understand, but then I see people’s faces and I realize I’ve gone somewhere else.\n\nLike if my cousin asks me something simple — “Did you pay that bill?” or “What time is the appointment?” — I might start answering, but then I’ll explain why the grant mattered, and then how the shipping routes connect to what was taken or moved, and then I’m talking about my grandmother’s altar because that’s where I first learned how things get preserved, and then somehow I’m back at the appointment but nobody knows what I meant anymore.\n\nIn my mind it feels connected. It doesn’t feel random while I’m saying it. Each piece leads to the next piece, like stepping stones. But I think other people don’t see the stones, so to them I sound like I’m wandering. And sometimes by the time I stop talking, I can’t even remember the original question exactly, or I know it was about something practical but I’ve made it into this whole… long explanation. My cousin will just look tired and say, “Can you just tell me yes or no?” and then I feel foolish, because I wasn’t trying to make it difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your disorganized thinking and behavior happen most of the time, even when you're not hearing voices or feeling pressured by them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does happen even when there aren’t voices pushing at me. It’s not only when I’m hearing something. Sometimes it’s just ordinary conversation, and I can feel myself trying to answer, but the answer sort of opens into too many rooms.\n\nLike with my cousin — she’ll ask something very plain, like whether I paid a bill or what time an appointment is, and I’ll mean to say yes or no, or give the time. But then I start explaining why the grant mattered, and then I’m talking about shipping routes and where things were moved, and then somehow I’m explaining my grandmother’s altar because that feels connected to preservation and memory, and by the time I stop, nobody seems to know what I meant.\n\nIt feels connected to me while I’m saying it. That’s the frustrating part. I’m not trying to be confusing. But I can see from people’s faces that I’ve lost the thread, or maybe they have. And then I feel ashamed because it was supposed to be a simple answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships or work due to these experiences and thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s… it’s caused real problems. I wish I could say it was only private, something that just happened in my head and then passed, but it hasn’t stayed contained like that.\n\nI’ve lost people’s trust, especially colleagues from the museum and the board. I used to be seen as someone who could keep things organized, who would show up prepared, who knew where records were and what needed to happen next. And now I’ve missed board meetings, or I’ve arrived in such a state that people didn’t know whether I was actually able to participate. There were public events where I thought I could manage it, and then afterward someone had to gently tell me I had confused people or made them uncomfortable, and that is very hard to hear.\n\nMoney and appointments have been bad too. I’ve missed bills — not because I didn’t care, but because I would put them somewhere “safe,” or I would start trying to sort out why the timing mattered, and then the actual payment just wouldn’t happen. My cousin has had to step in with things like appointments and bills, and a museum administrator also had to help manage some of the public-facing things because I wasn’t handling them safely on my own anymore.\n\nIt’s humiliating, honestly. I’m 64 years old. I have an education. I’m not someone who never knew how to manage a calendar or speak at an event. But lately I can’t reliably keep track of things, or know when I’m making a situation worse, and other people have had to take over pieces of my life that I used to manage myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
